**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: these are the owner's four disciplines in operation — precise reference, native subject, argued extension — and they are what convert a slogan into evidence.
- **B:** Incorrect: the claim of continuous inter-religious application is false, since the extension is a nineteenth- and twentieth-century development requiring argument.
- **C:** Incorrect: the verse is not treated as a disputed interpolation, and dismissing it would discard the strongest short Indian text for the pluralist case.
- **D:** Incorrect: vague citation is the specific fault the discipline note targets, and it signals that the candidate has met the line only as a slogan.

**Examiner trap 18:** The verse earns marks because it separates one referent from many namings, and that separation is exactly what breaks the inference from absolute truth to exclusivism; used as a decorative quotation it earns nothing.

#### MCQ 19. Vivekananda's demand, stated in his own terms

What does Vivekananda demand at the Parliament of the World's Religions, and why?

A. Tolerance, on the ground that a plural society cannot survive without a settled legal framework for the peaceful management of religious difference.

B. Doctrinal convergence, on the ground that the surviving differences between religions are verbal rather than substantive once analysed.

C. Institutional unification, on the ground that separate religious organisations inevitably compete for adherents and therefore generate conflict.

D. Acceptance rather than mere toleration, on the ground that toleration still condescends to the tradition it permits to exist.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: toleration is precisely what he regards as insufficient, so attributing that demand to him inverts his position.
- **B:** Incorrect: he preserves distinctive forms and denies homogenisation, so treating the differences as merely verbal misrepresents the model.
- **C:** Incorrect: institutional unification is not proposed; the model derives plural paths from plural temperaments and expects them to persist.
- **D:** Correct: this is his own emphasis at Chicago in 1893, and read against Forst's ladder it is a refusal of permission-level toleration.

**Examiner trap 19:** The 2025 stem names the thinker, so the mark scheme is fidelity to his own theses — the temperament argument, the four disciplines and the acceptance demand — rather than generic Vedāntic pluralism.

#### MCQ 20. The status of the assimilation objection

How should the charge that Vivekananda's model is covertly neo-Vedāntic inclusivism be handled?

A. Dismissed, because Vivekananda explicitly affirms that distinctive religious forms are preserved and that harmony does not require homogenisation.

B. Accepted, because any position that supplies a metaphysical ground for religious plurality is by definition an inclusivism about the traditions it grounds.

C. Stated with its reply and then left contested, since whether the metaphysical hierarchy remains inclusivist is not settled by the preservation of forms.

D. Deferred to the Advanced block, because the objection concerns interpretive controversy rather than the doctrine an examination answer requires.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: preservation of distinctive forms answers part of the charge but does not settle whether Vedānta supplies the final adjudicating framework.
- **B:** Incorrect: supplying a metaphysical ground is not by itself inclusivism, since a second-order account may claim to privilege no first-order tradition.
- **C:** Correct: the owner's instruction is to keep the status contested, and the Advanced dossier records the same objection and reply without resolving them.
- **D:** Incorrect: the objection is directly examinable in the 2025 question and belongs in the core answer rather than in optional enrichment.

**Examiner trap 20:** Declaring the assimilation objection solved is as costly as ignoring it; the mature move is to state the reply, name what it does not settle, and let the contested status stand as the answer's evaluative conclusion.

#### MCQ 21. Radhakrishnan's criterion and its characteristic risk

Which pairing correctly matches Radhakrishnan's criterion with the objection his position characteristically attracts?

A. Criterion: integral experience — Risk: perennialist reduction, in which doctrines are demoted to variant readings of one underlying experience.

B. Criterion: soteriological transformation — Risk: an empty Real, since the terminus of transformation cannot be described in any tradition's categories.

C. Criterion: conditional predication — Risk: hard contradictions, since some claims conflict on the same question in the same respect.

D. Criterion: fidelity to self-understanding — Risk: the surrender of a single ultimate reality behind the plurality of religious ends.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: the inter-thinker grid records integral experience as his criterion and perennialist reduction as his main objection.
- **B:** Incorrect: soteriological transformation and the empty-Real objection belong to Hick, whose criterion is practical rather than experiential in Radhakrishnan's sense.
- **C:** Incorrect: conditional predication and the residue of hard contradictions belong to the Jain approach, not to Radhakrishnan.
- **D:** Incorrect: fidelity to self-understanding with the loss of a single ultimate is Heim's position, and it is in fact the opposite of a perennialist reduction.

**Examiner trap 21:** Radhakrishnan and Vivekananda are routinely merged; their criteria differ — integral experience against realisation — and so do their recorded risks, and separating them demonstrates control that a general "Indian pluralism" paragraph cannot.

#### MCQ 22. Why conditional predication is not relativism

Why does the Jain apparatus of standpoint-indexed assertion avoid the charge of relativism?

A. Because it applies only to claims about material substances and their properties, leaving claims about liberation, karma and the soul entirely outside its declared scope.

B. Because the respects under which incompatible predicates are asserted are stated explicitly, so the assertions remain determinate and checkable.

C. Because it is a monastic discipline for debate rather than a philosophical doctrine about the truth-conditions of assertions.

D. Because it denies that finite knowers can make assertions at all, replacing assertion with a practice of principled silence.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: the apparatus is general in scope, and restricting it to material substances would remove exactly the applications the clause needs.
- **B:** Correct: relativism denies a fact of the matter, whereas conditional predication states the respect and thereby regulates rather than abandons predication.
- **C:** Incorrect: it is a doctrine about how assertions must be qualified, not merely a debating convention, which is why it functions as a logic of standpoints.
- **D:** Incorrect: assertion is not abandoned; it is indexed, and the sevenfold scheme is precisely an enumeration of the available modes of assertion.

**Examiner trap 22:** Describing the elephant parable is worth markedly less than stating the apparatus as a logic that marks framework-relativity inside the proposition, which is what makes it a genuine contribution to the deep-disagreement problem.

#### MCQ 23. The premise that carries the 2022 inference

The inference from "there is one Absolute Truth" to "religious exclusivism follows" is valid only if a further premise is supplied. Which premise is it?

A. That religious traditions in fact make contradictory claims about the nature of ultimate reality rather than merely appearing to do so.

B. That the state has an interest in the truth of religious doctrines and is therefore entitled to prefer one tradition over others.

C. That a finite formulation can exhaust the absolute, so that one tradition's statement of it can be the whole truth about it.

D. That religious experience is veridical, so that at least one tradition's reports about the absolute must be accepted as accurate.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: contradictory claims generate the problem but do not by themselves license exclusivism, since the contradiction could be resolved in favour of partial adequacy on all sides.
- **B:** Incorrect: this is a political premise about state action and belongs to a different axis; the inference under examination is alethic and epistemic.
- **C:** Correct: this is the suppressed premise, and Advaita's *nirguṇa* doctrine, Jain conditional predication and Hick's transcategorial Real each deny it independently.
- **D:** Incorrect: veridicality of experience is neither necessary nor sufficient for the inference, and denying it would not by itself block exclusivism.

**Examiner trap 23:** The 2022 stem is graded on whether the inference is tested rather than on whether the candidate agrees with it; concede the reading on which it succeeds, then deny the premise and the inference fails without any appeal to tolerance.

#### MCQ 24. The control on hard contradiction

Which statement expresses the hard-contradiction control correctly?

A. Contradictions between traditions are always apparent, since every apparent conflict can be resolved by distinguishing level, symbol, standpoint or soteriological end.

B. Pluralism may reinterpret level, symbol, standpoint or end, but must acknowledge whatever contradiction remains rather than harmonising it.

C. Contradictions between traditions show that at most one tradition is authentic, so pluralism is refuted wherever a genuine contradiction can be located.

D. Contradictions between traditions are irrelevant to pluralism, which is a thesis about salvation rather than a thesis about the truth of doctrines.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this overstates the four reinterpretive moves into a universal solvent, which is exactly the rhetorical harmonisation the control forbids.
- **B:** Correct: the four moves are legitimate where a difference of respect can be specified, and the residue must be acknowledged; this is the owner's control verbatim in substance.
- **C:** Incorrect: a contradiction shows that at most one party is right **about that claim**, which is compatible with soteriological and political pluralism elsewhere.
- **D:** Incorrect: pluralism has an alethic dimension as well as a soteriological one, so contradictions between doctrines are directly relevant to it.

**Examiner trap 24:** The top band explicitly rewards acknowledging the contradictions pluralism cannot dissolve; a script that harmonises everything is not more generous than one that concedes the residue, it is simply less defensible.

#### REMEDIAL DIAGNOSTIC MCQS — 8

#### MCQ 25. Peer disagreement against deep disagreement

What distinguishes deep disagreement in Fogelin's technical sense from ordinary peer disagreement?

A. Deep disagreement concerns religious questions specifically, whereas peer disagreement concerns questions in the empirical sciences.

B. Deep disagreement occurs between parties of unequal competence, whereas peer disagreement requires the parties to be equally competent.

C. Deep disagreement is about values while peer disagreement is about facts, so the two require different methods of resolution.

D. Deep disagreement obtains when the parties do not share the framework propositions against which evidence would count at all.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: the distinction is structural rather than topical, and Fogelin's analysis is not restricted to religious subject matter.
- **B:** Incorrect: inequality of competence is not the criterion; parties to a deep disagreement may be perfectly matched in ability and evidence.
- **C:** Incorrect: the fact–value distinction does not track the difference, since framework divergence can arise about matters of fact as readily as about values.
- **D:** Correct: this is Fogelin's criterion, connected to Wittgenstein's hinge propositions in *On Certainty*, and it is why the same evidence is not evidence for the same thing.

**Examiner trap 25:** Peer disagreement assumes a shared framework and asks what to do about a peer; deep disagreement denies the shared framework, and conflating them makes both the conciliationist and the steadfast position unstatable.

#### MCQ 26. What unresolvability does and does not show

If inter-religious disagreement is deep in the technical sense, what follows?

A. That relativism about religious truth is thereby established, since a disagreement that argument cannot settle can have no fact of the matter standing behind it.

B. That unresolvability is epistemic rather than alethic: one party may still be right, and what fails is our ability to demonstrate which.

C. That dialogue is pointless, since parties who share no framework cannot communicate about the matters on which they differ.

D. That pluralism is vindicated, since the persistence of disagreement is exactly what the pluralist hypothesis predicts.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this is the inference the epistemic/alethic distinction blocks, and the owner records it as the objection to be answered rather than the conclusion to be drawn.
- **B:** Correct: this is the reply verbatim in substance, and it is the discriminating move the top band names.
- **C:** Incorrect: the analysis licenses only the retention of belief, not the refusal of dialogue, and it makes the procedural virtues obligatory rather than optional.
- **D:** Incorrect: consequence three states explicitly that pluralism cannot be established merely by pointing at the persistence of disagreement.

**Examiner trap 26:** The four consequences of deep disagreement cut in both directions — they protect the exclusivist from a charge of irrationality and deny the pluralist an easy argument — and using only the half that suits the script is visible to an examiner.

#### MCQ 27. What makes an attitude toleration

Which set of conditions is jointly necessary for an attitude to count as toleration?

A. Objection to what is tolerated, reasons that outweigh but do not cancel the objection, and the power to interfere combined with deliberate restraint.

B. Indifference to what is tolerated, absence of any power to interfere, and a general disposition to avoid conflict with one's neighbours.

C. Approval of what is tolerated, a legal duty not to interfere, and a settled expectation that the other party will reciprocate in due course.

D. Uncertainty about what is tolerated, willingness to revise one's own view, and a commitment to submit the disagreement to public argument.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: objection, acceptance and power are the three components, and removing the first yields indifference while removing the third yields mere endurance.
- **B:** Incorrect: indifference removes the objection component, which is precisely what makes toleration a moral achievement rather than an absence of concern.
- **C:** Incorrect: approval removes the objection component in the other direction, and a legal duty removes the deliberate restraint that the power component requires.
- **D:** Incorrect: this describes fallibilism and a commitment to public reason, which are valuable but are not what toleration means.

**Examiner trap 27:** In the 2020 tolerance question the definition does the work; define toleration by its three components first, and the importance the stem asks about follows from the definition instead of having to be asserted.

#### MCQ 28. Grading a historical example on Forst's ladder

A ruling majority permits a religious minority to practise its faith on sufferance, reserving the right to withdraw the permission. Where does this fall on Forst's ladder, and what follows?

A. Esteem, since permitting practice acknowledges that the minority's beliefs contain something worthwhile.

B. Respect, since the minority is being recognised as entitled to practise its faith without interference.

C. Permission, which is what most historical toleration actually was and which is not compatible with equal citizenship.

D. Coexistence, since the arrangement is a pragmatic settlement designed to avoid conflict between the two communities.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: esteem requires positively valuing the other's beliefs, and a revocable sufferance expresses no such valuation.
- **B:** Incorrect: respect requires reciprocal recognition as equal moral-political persons, which a unilateral and revocable permission by definition withholds.
- **C:** Correct: sufferance granted unilaterally by the powerful is Forst's first conception, and only respect and esteem are compatible with equal citizenship.
- **D:** Incorrect: coexistence is mutual and pragmatic, whereas the arrangement described is asymmetrical and dependent on the majority's continuing choice.

**Examiner trap 28:** Grade every historical example on the ladder before praising it; the habit converts a list of tolerant rulers into an argument, and it is what allows Vivekananda's demand for acceptance to be located precisely rather than merely admired.

#### MCQ 29. The scope of Popper's paradox

How should Popper's paradox of tolerance be stated in an examination answer?

A. As a general principle that a society may suppress any doctrine which the majority of its citizens judges to be false or harmful.

B. As the claim that unlimited tolerance destroys tolerance, with the right to refuse tolerance reserved for those who answer argument with violence.

C. As an argument that tolerance is impossible in a plural society, since every act of toleration presupposes a power that will eventually be abused.

D. As a rejection of Mill's harm principle, on the ground that harm is too vague a criterion to bear the weight of political limitation.

**Answer: B.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this is the misuse the owner's trap list targets, since limits drawn by doctrinal content reproduce the intolerance they were meant to prevent.
- **B:** Correct: Popper's own formulation in the note to chapter 7 reserves the refusal for those who refuse rational argument and answer with violence.
- **C:** Incorrect: the paradox is a limit on tolerance rather than an argument for its impossibility, and Popper is defending the open society rather than despairing of it.
- **D:** Incorrect: the paradox is not a rejection of the harm principle; harm and reciprocity are precisely the criteria by which the limits should be drawn.

**Examiner trap 29:** Quoting Popper as a licence to suppress the intolerant is the single most misused citation in this clause; state the restriction in the same sentence as the paradox, and the citation becomes an argument rather than a slogan.

#### MCQ 30. The hardest problem in religious freedom

Which problem does the owner identify as the single most examinable tension in the religious-freedom question?

A. Whether the state should fund religious institutions on the same terms as comparable secular institutions.

B. Whether freedom of conscience extends to the refusal of medical treatment on religious grounds.

C. Whether group rights granted to protect a community can entrench its dominant members over its own weaker members.

D. Whether propagation should be permitted in public spaces or restricted to premises owned by the propagating community.

**Answer: C.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: funding questions are downstream of the neutrality models and are not identified as the central tension.
- **B:** Incorrect: this is an instance of the practice/belief asymmetry, which is a listed hard problem but not the one singled out.
- **C:** Correct: this is Shachar's paradox of multicultural vulnerability, which the owner records as the single most examinable tension in the clause.
- **D:** Incorrect: the propagation question is a listed hard problem whose principled line is coercion and fraud, but it is not the one identified as central.

**Examiner trap 30:** Answering a religious-freedom question with a list of constitutional articles leaves the philosophy untouched; the marks are in the asymmetry, the conversion line, the internal-minorities paradox, the two neutrality models and the state-as-arbiter-of-theology problem.

#### MCQ 31. The analytic move in the conflict-history question

Which distinction is the one that earns marks in answering whether the history of religions is the history of conflicts?

A. Religion as cause, as marker of an antagonism generated otherwise, as mobiliser of grievance, and as legitimiser of a decision taken on other grounds.

B. Religion as ancient and as modern, since pre-modern conflicts were fought over territory and dynastic succession while modern ones are fought over group identity.

C. Religion as institutional and as personal, since institutions generate conflict whereas individual belief is politically inert.

D. Religion as monotheistic and as non-monotheistic, since exclusive truth-claims arise only where a single deity is affirmed.

**Answer: A.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Correct: this is the four-role distinction the owner identifies as the analytic move that earns marks, and most historical cases fall into the last three roles.
- **B:** Incorrect: a chronological split is the kind of periodisation Armstrong's argument complicates, and it does not isolate religion as a variable.
- **C:** Incorrect: the institutional/personal split does not track the causal question, and treating personal belief as inert begs the question at issue.
- **D:** Incorrect: exclusive truth-claims are not confined to monotheism, and the classification would prejudge the causal question by construction.

**Examiner trap 31:** A list of wars answers a different question from the one printed; grade the claim into its three versions, apply the four roles, then supply the counter-evidence, since a history that counts only the wars is a selected history.

#### MCQ 32. The shape of the answer on religion as a unifier

Which verdict on religion as a uniting force in a globalising world matches what the directive is buying?

A. Religion unites, because every major tradition contains a version of the golden rule and a doctrine of universal moral standing.

B. Religion divides, because Durkheimian integration is in-group integration and globalisation produces reactive and fundamentalist forms as readily as cosmopolitan ones.

C. The question cannot be answered at all, because "religion" is not a stable independent variable that can be isolated from politics, land, economics and group identity.

D. Religion unites conditionally: when its ethics are universalist in scope, its epistemology fallibilist enough for dialogue, and its identity not politically monopolised.

**Answer: D.**

**Option explanations:**

- **A:** Incorrect: this states one side of the evidence as though it were the verdict, and it cannot explain why the same traditions also produce communal violence.
- **B:** Incorrect: this states the other side as the verdict and is refuted by the same symmetry, since the traditions cited also produced the Parliament of Religions.
- **C:** Incorrect: the deconstruction of religion as a variable is a move within the conflict-history question, and using it here evades a directive that asks for a verdict.
- **D:** Correct: the directive decoder requires a conditional verdict with the conditions named, and these are the three conditions the owner's synthesis states.

**Examiner trap 32:** A conditional verdict is only an answer if the conditions are specified and independently checkable; "it depends" without the three conditions reads as evasion, while the three conditions read as analysis.

## PYQS AND ANSWER PRACTICE

### Verified previous-year questions — 12 complete solutions

**Word bands used throughout.** 10 marks ≈ 150 words · 15 marks ≈ 250 words · 20 marks ≈ 350 words. Every question below is reproduced in its **exact printed wording** from the audited Philosophy of Religion previous-year ledger, 2018–2025, in which this clause owns **twelve question-parts out of 112** — two in each of 2018, 2019, 2020 and 2021, and one in each of 2022, 2023, 2024 and 2025. **The model answers are independent learner practice and are never official UPSC keys.**

**Timing skeleton.** 10 marks — 1 minute to decode, 1 to outline, 6 to write, 1 to check. 15 marks — 1 / 2 / 10 / 2. 20 marks — 2 / 3 / 13 / 2. **Standing penalties in this clause:** defining pluralism as "all religions say the same thing"; merging inclusivism with pluralism; presenting exclusivism only as bigotry; citing *ekaṃ sat* without its context or its bridging argument; quoting *extra ecclesiam nulla salus* as Cyprian's own words; treating "tolerance" as one thing; answering the conflict-history stem with a list of wars; and citing Huntington as a finding.

#### PYQ 1 — 2018, Q6(a), 20 marks

**Exact verified question:** Distinguish between Exclusivism, Inclusivism and Pluralism with regard to the conflicting truth-claims of different religions.

**Demand decoding:** "Distinguish A, B and C with regard to X" buys **three clean differentiae on one axis**. Fix the axis before distinguishing — here the printed axis is *conflicting truth-claims*, so truth is primary and salvation is secondary. A narrative of three thinkers will not score; a three-way differentia will.

**Model solution:**
The three positions differ on a single question: **who sets the terms on which another tradition's claims are judged?**

**Exclusivism** holds that one revelation carries uniquely decisive truth and salvific authority, so where others contradict it they are mistaken. It appeals to determinate revelation and non-contradiction. Its classical soteriological expression is *extra ecclesiam nulla salus* — a later standard phrasing of Cyprian's *salus extra ecclesiam non est* (*Ep.* 73), hardened at Lateran IV (1215), *Unam Sanctam* (1302) and Florence (1442), then decisively qualified by *Lumen Gentium* 16 and *Nostra Aetate* (1965). Its strength is that it preserves determinate content; its weakness is privileged warrant, sharpened by the contingency of birth. It does not entail intolerance: Locke's argument that force cannot produce belief is available to it.

**Inclusivism** grants other traditions **partial truth** and possible salvation, but interprets that success through the home tradition — Rahner's "anonymous Christians". Others are included **on terms they have not set**; the standard does not move, only the boundary. Its cost is asymmetry: the home tradition is never redescribed in the other's categories.

**Pluralism** denies that any finite tradition exhaustively possesses the absolute. Hick's identist version treats the traditions as culturally conditioned responses to the transcategorial Real, assessed by transformation from self-centredness to Reality-centredness; Heim's differential version holds that communion, non-dual realisation and cessation are three destinations rather than three descriptions. It is not relativism, indifferentism, syncretism or "all religions say the same thing".

Plantinga defends exclusivism by parity — the pluralist's own denial is contested too — while D'Costa charges pluralism with covert exclusivism. The triad is therefore a map, not a verdict.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Fixes one axis at the outset, which is exactly what the directive buys.
- States each position by **who sets the terms**, giving three clean differentiae rather than three summaries.
- Carries the full attribution chain on the Latin tag, including the 1965 qualification.
- Distinguishes two varieties of pluralism, which most scripts treat as one.
- Closes with the parity exchange, converting classification into assessment.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** the compressible part is the conciliar chain — at 350 words, keep Cyprian's wording and the 1965 qualification and drop the three council dates. What must never be cut is the "who sets the terms" formula, because it is the differentia the directive is grading.

#### PYQ 2 — 2018, Q6(b), 15 marks

**Exact verified question:** "Truth is one, yet people perceive differently." Critically evaluate by considering the present Indian context.

**Demand decoding:** A quotation **plus** a context clause. The clause carries marks, so an Indian application is compulsory. "Critically evaluate" requires the quotation to be tested, not endorsed.

**Model solution:**
The statement distinguishes a single truth from plural perception: an absolute **referent** with perspectival **access**. Its strongest Indian anchor is *ekaṃ sad viprā bahudhā vadanti*, **Ṛgveda 1.164.46**, from the *asya vāmasya* hymn attributed to Dīrghatamas. Yet its native subject is the naming of Vedic deities; extending it to world religions is a modern Vivekananda–Radhakrishnan–Gandhi argument, not the verse's unmediated meaning.

Advaita supports the structure through Brahman **without qualities** (*nirguṇa*), beyond exhaustive predication, and **with qualities** (*saguṇa*) forms. Jain many-sidedness (*anekāntavāda*) adds epistemic discipline: standpoint theory (*nayavāda*) and conditional predication (*syādvāda*) index assertions to a respect, rather than making every view true.

The statement therefore supports humility and equal respect in India, but not "all religions say the same thing." Gandhi's fallibilism and Sen's warning against singular affiliation explain its civic relevance. Hard contradictions nevertheless remain: a personal creator cannot both exist and not exist in the same respect. The maxim is defensible only as one referent with partial, corrigible access.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Reads the quotation as referent-versus-access rather than paraphrasing it.
- Applies the full citation discipline to RV 1.164.46, which the strong band requires.
- Uses *nirguṇa*/*saguṇa* and Jain many-sidedness as machinery, not as decoration.
- Answers the context clause with a determinate Indian application, including Sen.
- Concedes the hard-contradiction limit, which is what "critically" is buying.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 250 words, compress the Jain apparatus to one sentence naming *syāt* and standpoint-indexing. What must never be cut is the context clause, since omitting the Indian application forfeits marks the directive explicitly allocates.

#### PYQ 3 — 2019, Q5(b), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** Is religion a uniting force for humanity in the globalizing world as of today? Discuss.

**Demand decoding:** A "uniting force" stem buys a **conditional verdict with the conditions stated up front**. Structure: for / against / conditions / verdict. Neither optimism nor cynicism scores.

**Model solution:**
Religion is a conditional, not intrinsic, unifier. Durkheim shows how shared rites create collective effervescence and a moral community; the 1893 and 1993 Parliaments of the World's Religions and Hans Küng's *Declaration Toward a Global Ethic* illustrate transreligious cooperation.

Yet Durkheimian integration is also in-group demarcation. Huntington's clash thesis must be treated as a criticised thesis, while globalisation can generate both cosmopolitan networks and reactive identities. Religious symbols are also easily politically instrumentalised.

Religion unites humanity only when three conditions hold: its ethics are universalist, its epistemology is fallibilist enough for dialogue, and its identity is not politically monopolised. It is therefore a high-amplitude social force: the same traditions can sustain interfaith solidarity or communal division, depending on those conditions.

**Why this earns marks:**
- States the verdict form in the first line, so the structure is visible immediately.
- Uses Durkheim on both sides, which is what makes the citation analytical.
- Handles Huntington with the required qualification.
- Names three checkable conditions rather than gesturing at complexity.
- Ends with the amplitude formula, the clause's signature line for this stem.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, cut the list of universalist doctrines to one example and compress Roy to a clause. What must never be cut is the three-condition synthesis, because the conditions *are* the answer the directive is buying.

#### PYQ 4 — 2019, Q7(a), 20 marks

**Exact verified question:** Expound and explain the central problem in the discussion between religious pluralists and religious exclusivists.

**Demand decoding:** "The **central** problem" is singular. Name it in one sentence, then unfold it. A survey of both positions without an isolated central issue answers a different question.

**Model solution:**
The central problem is this: **is there any standpoint from which the competing claims of the traditions can be adjudicated without already occupying one of them?** Everything else in the dispute follows from that.

The exclusivist says no neutral standpoint is needed, because one revelation carries decisive authority; determinate truth and non-contradiction do the rest. The pluralist says a second-order account is available which privileges no first-order tradition: Hick relocates the centre to the transcategorial Real by four steps — persistent, birth-linked diversity; transformation produced across traditions; equal divine concern resisting arbitrary exclusivism; therefore one Real experienced through different conceptual schemes.

The problem bites in both directions. **Plantinga** answers the charges of arbitrariness, arrogance and irrationality by parity: the pluralist too holds a contested belief that the exclusivist rejects; believing what others deny is the ordinary condition of every philosopher; and awareness of disagreement may reduce warrant without extinguishing it. That establishes permissibility, not truth, and it leaves the contingency of birth untouched.

**D'Costa** answers from the other side: pluralism makes its own second-order account uniquely true and reinterprets traditions against their self-understanding — a covert exclusivism. Hick's reply, that his is a philosophical hypothesis rather than a rival creed, does not fully escape the charge, since a theory that tells traditions what they are *really* doing exercises the authority it denies them.

Underneath both lies **deep disagreement** in Fogelin's sense: the parties do not share the framework propositions against which evidence would count, so the failure of convergence is predicted rather than scandalous. It neither convicts exclusivism of irrationality nor establishes pluralism.

The central problem is therefore not which side is more generous but whether neutrality is available. It is not; what is available is corrigibility — fallibilism, humility, charity, and making one's framework explicit.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Names the central problem in a single sentence before unfolding it, exactly as the directive requires.
- Reconstructs Hick's argument in numbered steps rather than describing it.
- Gives Plantinga and D'Costa as a symmetrical pair, which is what makes the problem central rather than partisan.
- Adds the deep-disagreement layer, which the verdict ladder places in the strong band.
- Converts the stalemate into a determinate conclusion about corrigibility.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 350 words, compress Hick's four steps to two clauses. What must never be cut is the pairing of Plantinga with D'Costa, since a script with only one of them reads as advocacy rather than as an account of a *problem*.

#### PYQ 5 — 2020, Q5(c), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** Is it acceptable that the History of Religions is the History of Conflicts? Discuss.

**Demand decoding:** A sweeping historical claim. **Grade it into versions**, refute the strong one, concede the narrow one. A list of wars answers a different question.

**Model solution:**
The claim has three strengths: religions have been **involved** in conflicts; religion has sometimes been a **significant cause**; religion is the **defining engine** of conflict. The first is trivial, the third false, and only the second needs case-by-case proof.

Exclusivist certainty, sacralised authority and singular group identity can generate conflict; Kimball's markers are a useful typology, not a law. But Cavanaugh argues that "religious violence" depends on a modern religious/secular distinction; Armstrong warns against projecting that distinction backwards; Sen locates danger in the singular-affiliation fallacy.

Hence distinguish religion as **cause, marker, mobiliser or legitimiser**. Most cases combine political, economic and territorial factors. Counter-evidence—Aśoka's concord, Bhakti-Sufi universalism, abolitionism, civil rights and *satyāgraha*—also matters. The thesis is historically selective, though exclusivist certainty joined to monopolised identity remains genuinely dangerous.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Grades the claim in the first sentence, which is the structural consequence the directive demands.
- States the case *for* fairly before refuting it.
- Deploys the four-role distinction, which the owner identifies as the mark-earning move.
- Supplies counter-evidence, making the refutation historical as well as conceptual.
- Concedes a narrow truth, which is what "discuss" is buying.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, reduce the counter-evidence list to three items. What must never be cut is the four-role distinction, because without it the answer becomes the list of wars the trap list forbids.

#### PYQ 6 — 2020, Q5(e), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** What is the importance of religious tolerance in a multicultural pluralistic society? Justify your answer.

**Demand decoding:** "Importance … justify" means the importance must be **argued**, not asserted. Define tolerance properly first: the definition does the work.

**Model solution:**
Tolerance is not indifference. It requires **objection**, overriding **acceptance reasons**, and the **power** to interfere combined with restraint. Its paradox—allowing what one believes wrong—is resolved by separating first-order disagreement from second-order reasons against coercion.

Its importance follows from those reasons. Locke argues that force cannot produce belief; Mill adds harm and the epistemic cost of suppression. Aśoka's Rock Edict XII forbids disparaging other sects and commends concord (*samavāya*); Gandhi adds fallibilist equal respect, while Vivekananda demands acceptance rather than condescending toleration.

Forst's ladder—permission, coexistence, respect, esteem—shows the limit: tolerance granted by the strong may remain permission. It is therefore indispensable for peaceful disagreement but insufficient for equality; a plural society needs respect-based toleration bounded by harm and reciprocity.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Defines by three components, so the importance is derived rather than asserted.
- States the paradox and resolves it by levels, which is the philosophical core.
- Gives both argument-stocks, with Aśoka correctly identified as the earliest verifiable state-level argument.
- Applies Forst's ladder as a grading instrument rather than a list.
- Closes with the indispensable-but-insufficient verdict, naming both reasons.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, keep the three components, Locke, Aśoka and the ladder; compress Mill, Rawls and Popper into one clause. What must never be cut is the power component, since the closing verdict depends on it.

#### PYQ 7 — 2021, Q5(b), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** Discuss the possibility of Absolute Truth in the context of religious pluralism.

**Demand decoding:** A **possibility** claim. The examiner is buying the **compatibility mechanism**: absolute referent with perspectival access. Show the mechanism, do not merely assert the compatibility.

**Model solution:**
Absolute Truth is compatible with religious pluralism once four questions are separated: **referent, formulation, epistemic access and salvation**. Pluralism need not deny an absolute referent; it denies that any finite formulation exhausts it.

The mechanism is ontological unity without epistemic monopoly. Advaita's Brahman without qualities (*nirguṇa*) exceeds exhaustive predication while with-qualities (*saguṇa*) forms guide devotion. Jain conditional predication (*syādvāda*) indexes assertions to standpoints, and Hick calls the Real transcategorial. These are distinct ways of rejecting the suppressed premise that one finite statement can possess the absolute completely.

The view assumes that truth permits partial adequacy or non-literal mediation. It also cannot dissolve direct contradiction in the same respect. Absolute Truth is therefore possible as one inexhaustible referent approached fallibly, not as one finite formulation held with unquestionable certainty.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Separates referent, formulation, access and salvation, which is the owner's instruction for every absolute-truth stem.
- Exhibits the compatibility **mechanism**, which is what a possibility directive buys.
- Cites three independent denials of the suppressed premise, one Western and two Indian.
- Names the presupposition the position itself makes.
- Concedes the hard-contradiction residue in the same answer.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, keep the four-way separation and one denial of the premise. What must never be cut is the closing distinction between a single referent inexhaustibly formulated and a finite formulation held absolutely, since that sentence is the possibility claim.

#### PYQ 8 — 2021, Q5(c), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** Is religious freedom possible in a multireligious society? Explain.

**Demand decoding:** Feasibility, **with the hard cases faced**. Naming rights without facing the practice/belief asymmetry and the internal-minorities problem will not reach the upper bands.

**Model solution:**
Religious freedom is possible—and especially necessary—in a multireligious society, but it cannot be unlimited or perfectly neutral. It includes conscience, profession, practice, propagation, change or exit, freedom from compelled observance, and community association.

Locke's incoercibility argument is decisive: coercion produces profession, not belief. Autonomy and deep disagreement add reasons against state certainty. Yet belief and practice differ: practice may be limited by public order, morality, health, harm and reciprocity. Propagation protects persuasion, not coercion or fraud.

The hardest issue is Shachar's **multicultural vulnerability**: group protection can empower dominant insiders over women or dissenters. Bhargava's **principled distance** therefore improves on both strict separation and religious establishment by permitting differential engagement for freedom and equality. Religious freedom is feasible only with individual exit, internal-minority safeguards and justified limits on harmful practice.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Separates the bundle, so that "possible" can be answered differently for different members.
- Gives four independent justifications rather than one.
- Faces all the hard cases, including the one the owner calls the most examinable tension.
- Contrasts two neutrality models and explains what principled distance permits.
- Ends with a qualified verdict that names the residual cost.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, compress the four justifications to Locke plus one. What must never be cut is the internal-minorities paradox, since it is the discriminator between an average and a good script on this stem.

#### PYQ 9 — 2022, Q7(a), 20 marks

**Exact verified question:** "An unquestionable acceptance of only one Absolute Truth will inevitably result in religious exclusivism." Discuss.

**Demand decoding:** A quotation whose **inference** is to be tested, not its mood. The word doing the work is "inevitably". Show that ontological absolutism does not entail epistemic exclusivism.

**Model solution:**
The claim asserts an entailment. Reconstructed, it runs: there is exactly one Absolute Truth; a finite formulation can exhaust it; my tradition's formulation is that one; therefore every other tradition is simply mistaken. The argument is valid. It is unsound if either suppressed premise fails.

On one reading the inference does succeed, and that must be conceded first. If "absolute truth" means *my religion's propositions are the sole truth*, exclusivism follows immediately — indeed trivially, because exclusivism has been built into the premise.

But one may hold that there **is** an Absolute Truth — the Real, or Brahman — while recognising all religions as **partial and perspectival** approaches to it: an absolute **referent** with pluralist **access**. That breaks the link. Three traditions deny the suppressed premise independently. Advaita's Brahman **without qualities** (*nirguṇa*) is beyond exhaustive predication, while **with-qualities** (*saguṇa*) forms structure real devotional paths. Jain **many-sidedness** holds reality many-sided and every viewpoint partially true, with conditional predication (*syādvāda*) indexing assertions to a standpoint. Hick's Real is transcategorial, so personal and non-personal predicates attach to manifestations rather than to the Real itself.

Ontological absolutism, epistemic fallibilism and political tolerance are three separate theses. Establishing the first settles neither of the others — which is why an exclusivist may consistently defend religious freedom on Locke's ground that force cannot produce belief, and why tolerance cannot be inferred from metaphysics without an additional premise.

Two concessions keep the answer honest. The reply presupposes that truth admits degrees of adequacy or non-literal mediation. And hard contradictions remain: a personal creator either exists or does not, and pluralism must acknowledge that residue rather than harmonise it.

The entailment therefore fails. Ontological absolutism entails epistemic exclusivism only if one adds the premise that a finite formulation can exhaust the absolute — and that premise is exactly what Advaita's *nirguṇa* and Jain many-sidedness deny.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Reconstructs the argument formally and locates the suppressed premises, which is what "discuss" a claim means.
- Concedes the reading on which the inference succeeds before denying the other.
- Supplies three independent denials of the premise, two of them Indian.
- Separates the three theses explicitly, blocking the tolerance-from-metaphysics slide.
- Concedes both limits, which is what the top band rewards.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 350 words, compress the three denials to Advaita plus Jainism. What must never be cut is the formal reconstruction, since the whole answer depends on exhibiting a premise the quotation leaves unstated.

#### PYQ 10 — 2023, Q8(b), 15 marks

**Exact verified question:** Does religious pluralism invite inter-religious conflicts and destroy the truth of religion? Discuss.

**Demand decoding:** **Two allegations, answered separately.** Conflict-generation and truth-destruction are different charges, and a single undifferentiated reply loses half the marks.

**Model solution:**
The stem makes two distinct charges.

**Conflict:** pluralism may appear to weaken commitment and provoke reaction, but plurality alone does not cause violence. The better analysis distinguishes religion as **cause, marker, mobiliser and legitimiser**. Sen's singular-affiliation critique shows how monopolised identity, political power and exclusivist certainty mediate conflict. Political coexistence requires reciprocity and harm-limitation, not agreement about truth.

**Truth:** pluralism does not have to equalise propositions. Hick relocates absoluteness in the transcategorial Real and interprets traditions as culturally conditioned responses; this distinguishes equal dignity or salvific efficacy from identical propositional truth. Thus it denies exhaustive possession, not truth itself.

Two limits remain. Direct contradictions—such as creator and no creator in the same respect—cannot both be true; they must be routed through level, symbol, standpoint or different end, with residue acknowledged. Further, D'Costa argues that pluralism redescribes traditions through its own superior meta-framework and is therefore covertly exclusivist.

Pluralism need neither invite conflict nor destroy truth, but only if it retains non-contradiction, respects self-description and refuses to infer metaphysics from civic tolerance.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Splits the two allegations explicitly, which the directive requires.
- Answers the conflict charge with the four-role analysis rather than with reassurance.
- Answers the truth charge with the dignity-versus-truth distinction, which is the recorded reply.
- Concedes the hard-contradiction residue and the identity objection.
- Ends with the relocation formula and its qualification.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 250 words, compress the four-role analysis to a single clause. What must never be cut is the explicit separation of the two charges, since a merged answer forfeits the structure the directive is grading.

#### PYQ 11 — 2024, Q5(b), 10 marks

**Exact verified question:** Write a note on the notion of absolute truth in the context of religion.

**Demand decoding:** A **notion** question. It asks for the concept to be analysed, not for a verdict on a controversy. The four-way separation *is* the analysis.

**Model solution:**
"Absolute truth" in religion is truth claimed to be unconditioned by person, period or perspective. Four distinctions clarify the notion.

**Referent:** the Real, Brahman or God may be ontologically absolute. **Formulation:** every statement of that referent uses a language and conceptual scheme, so the statement need not be exhaustive. Advaita marks this through Brahman without qualities (*nirguṇa*) and with qualities (*saguṇa*). **Access:** finite knowers may grasp aspects rather than totality; Jain conditional predication (*syādvāda*) indexes claims to a standpoint. **Salvation:** whether possession of truth exclusively liberates is a further question.

Thus ontological absolutism does not entail epistemic monopoly or political privilege. The notion remains coherent if absoluteness belongs primarily to the referent, finite formulations remain corrigible, and contradictions surviving reinterpretation are acknowledged rather than harmonised.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Treats the stem as conceptual analysis, which is what "write a note on the notion" asks for.
- Gives four distinctions, each with a named tradition attached.
- Draws two consequences instead of leaving the analysis inert.
- Uses *nirguṇa*/*saguṇa* as a tradition's own internal marking of the point.
- Closes with the discipline that keeps absoluteness on the referent.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 150 words, drop one of the two consequences. What must never be cut is the four-way separation, since it is the analysis the notion-question is asking for.

#### PYQ 12 — 2025, Q7(a), 20 marks

**Exact verified question:** How does the Vedāntic view of Religious Pluralism address the conflicting truth claims of different faiths? Answer with reference to Swami Vivekananda's view of Universal Religion.

**Demand decoding:** "**How does** X address…? Answer with reference to [thinker]" buys a **mechanism** plus **fidelity to the named thinker**. Generic Vedānta does not score; Vivekananda's own theses do.

**Model solution:**
The Vedāntic response addresses conflicting truth-claims by a single move: it separates the **one referent** from the **many formulations**, and then explains the plurality of formulations by the plurality of human temperaments.

The textual anchor is *ekaṃ sad viprā bahudhā vadanti*, **Ṛgveda 1.164.46**, from the *asya vāmasya* riddle-hymn ascribed to Dīrghatamas. Discipline is required: its native subject is the naming of Vedic deities, and the inter-religious extension is a nineteenth- and twentieth-century development that must be argued. What the verse supplies is the structure — one existent, many namings.

Vivekananda's **Universal Religion** supplies the mechanism. Human capacities vary; spiritual disciplines must therefore vary; the non-dual ground supplies a common metaphysical reference; and the test is **realisation**, not doctrinal uniformity. The four disciplines — knowledge (*jñāna*), devotion (*bhakti*), action (*karma*) and meditative discipline (*rāja*) — address distinct dispositions. Plurality of path is therefore *expected*, not merely tolerated. At the Parliament of the World's Religions in Chicago in 1893 he demands **acceptance, not mere toleration**, on the ground that toleration still condescends.

Conflicting claims are handled at two levels: Brahman **without qualities** (*nirguṇa*) is beyond exhaustive predication, while **with-qualities** (*saguṇa*) forms structure real devotional paths. Jain many-sidedness adds an epistemic discipline — every judgement is standpoint-indexed — and so dogmatic exclusivism is dissolved without abandoning truth.

Two evaluations are required. Against Hick, the Vedāntic model grounds pluralism metaphysically rather than in a transcategorial Real that is arguably too thin to describe. Against Heim, it owes an argument: it does not follow without proof that traditions seeking personal communion, creator relation or cessation all reach the same end. And the standing objection is assimilation — if Vedānta supplies the final meta-framework, other faiths are included on terms they have not set, which is inclusivism. Vivekananda answers that distinctive forms are preserved and harmony does not require homogenisation; whether the metaphysical hierarchy remains inclusivist is **contested**.

The Vedāntic view therefore addresses conflicting truth-claims by relocating absoluteness in the referent and deriving plurality from temperament — a strong and specifically Indian pluralism whose unresolved question is whether its own framework is neutral.

**Why this earns marks:**
- Names the mechanism in the first sentence, which is what "how does" buys.
- Applies the full citation discipline to RV 1.164.46 rather than quoting it as a slogan.
- Uses Vivekananda's own theses — temperaments, the four disciplines, acceptance over toleration.
- Places the model against Hick and Heim, which converts exposition into assessment.
- Leaves the assimilation objection contested, as the owner requires.

**Upgrade route for this answer:** at 350 words, compress the Jain addition to one clause. What must never be cut is the assimilation objection with its reply and its contested status, since that is the evaluative move the 20-mark band is grading.

### Original solved Mains practice — 6 models

These six questions are **learner-written practice**, not previous-year questions. They are constructed to drill the demands the printed corpus has not yet combined.

#### Original 1 · 10 marks

**Question:** Distinguish between alethic and soteriological religious pluralism, and state one consequence of the distinction. *(Answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:**
Alethic pluralism concerns the **truth** of religious claims: it holds that several traditions state genuine truths about ultimate reality, because no finite formulation exhausts what it formulates. Soteriological pluralism concerns **efficacy**: it holds that several traditions really transform, liberate or save their adherents.

The two are separable in both directions. One may hold that another tradition's doctrines are largely mistaken while conceding that its practice transforms; one may hold that its doctrines are substantially true while denying that its path is efficacious for salvation.

The consequence is decisive for the standard argument. Hick's soteriological criterion — transformation from self-centredness to Reality-centredness — assesses traditions for authenticity and, by his own statement, does **not** render contradictory doctrines jointly true. A script that treats the criterion as settling the truth question has conceded the relativism charge without argument, since it has answered an alethic objection with soteriological evidence.

**Why this earns marks:** It defines both axes, demonstrates their logical independence through two counter-directions, and uses Hick's named criterion to show the precise examination consequence without confusing efficacy with truth.

#### Original 2 · 10 marks

**Question:** "Toleration granted by the strong to the weak is permission, not equality." Examine. *(Answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model answer:**
The statement is correct, and its force comes from the **power component** in the concept of toleration itself.

Toleration requires three things: an objection to what is tolerated, reasons that outweigh without cancelling that objection, and the power to interfere combined with deliberate restraint. The third condition means that toleration is always exercised by someone who *could* have done otherwise.

Forst's ladder makes the consequence explicit. **Permission** is the powerful allowing a minority to exist on sufferance; **coexistence** is a pragmatic modus vivendi; **respect** is reciprocal recognition as equal moral-political persons; **esteem** positively values the other's beliefs. Only respect and esteem are compatible with equal citizenship, and most historical toleration was permission.

Vivekananda reaches the same conclusion from the other direction, demanding acceptance rather than toleration because toleration still condescends. Toleration is thus indispensable — the objection component makes it a moral achievement — and insufficient.

**Why this earns marks:** It derives the conclusion from the power component, applies Forst's four-level distinction, adds Vivekananda as an independent Indian comparator, and ends with a qualified rather than celebratory verdict.

#### Original 3 · 15 marks

**Question:** Assess the claim that Hick's pluralistic hypothesis is a covert exclusivism. *(Answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:**
D'Costa's charge is the strongest single objection to Hick. Hick treats major traditions as culturally conditioned responses to the transcategorial Real, judged by transformation from self-centredness to Reality-centredness. He calls this a **second-order** philosophical hypothesis, not another creed.

D'Costa replies that the second-order theory is still presented as uniquely true and redescribes every tradition against its own self-understanding. Hick's distinction of levels therefore relocates privileged authority rather than abolishing it: a theory that tells traditions what they are "really" doing performs the adjudication it denies to them. The identity and mission objection confirms this cost.

Two qualifications matter. First, the criticism is diagnostic, not constructive: lack of neutrality does not establish exclusivism, and Plantinga's parity defence proves at most that exclusivist belief may remain rational. Second, not every pluralism bears the charge equally. Heim's differential pluralism treats communion, non-dual realisation and cessation as different real ends, preserving self-description better than Hick, though surrendering one ultimate destination.

Hickian pluralism is therefore not neutral, but neither is its rival. The decisive standard is whether the framework is explicit, corrigible and responsive to the traditions it interprets.

**Why this earns marks:** It reconstructs D'Costa's objection fairly, tests Hick's second-order reply, distinguishes diagnosis from proof of exclusivism, and compares Heim to show that the objection does not strike every pluralism equally.

#### Original 4 · 15 marks

**Question:** How far does Jain many-sidedness (*anekāntavāda*) resolve the problem of conflicting religious truth-claims? *(Answer in about 250 words.)*

**Model answer:**
It resolves a substantial part of the problem and leaves a residue that must be conceded.

The apparatus is three-tiered. **Many-sidedness** (*anekāntavāda*) holds reality to be many-sided; **standpoint theory** (*nayavāda*) makes explicit the standpoint from which a judgement is made; **conditional predication** (*syādvāda*) prefixes assertions with *syāt*, "in a certain respect", and the **sevenfold scheme** (*saptabhaṅgī*) enumerates the modes. Framework-relativity is thereby marked **inside** the proposition rather than left implicit.

This is not relativism. Relativism denies that there is a fact of the matter; conditional predication states the respect under which a predicate holds, so the assertions remain determinate and checkable. Predication is regulated, not abandoned.

Its power appears at two points. In the absolute-truth debate it denies the premise that a finite formulation can exhaust the absolute, so belief in one absolute Truth no longer entails exclusivism. In the epistemology of disagreement it addresses Fogelin's problem directly: deep disagreement arises because parties do not share the framework against which evidence would count, and the Jain device makes that framework explicit — a genuine Indian contribution to a live Western problem, and worth far more than the parable of the elephant.

Its limit is exact. The apparatus works wherever a difference of aspect, level or soteriological orientation can be specified. Where two claims contradict on the same question in the same respect — a personal creator exists, or does not — no index of standpoint helps. There, pluralism must distinguish literal conflict, symbolic language and different soteriological aims, and acknowledge whatever contradiction survives.

**Why this earns marks:** It explains the full Jain apparatus in English-first form, shows two distinct applications, rejects the relativist caricature, and concedes the exact same-respect contradiction that limits the doctrine.

#### Original 5 · 20 marks

**Question:** "Political coexistence between religions neither requires nor follows from agreement about religious truth." Critically discuss with reference to both Western and Indian resources. *(Answer in about 350 words.)*

**Model answer:**
The statement contains two claims, and both are defensible.

**Coexistence does not require truth-agreement.** Locke's *Letter Concerning Toleration* (1689) argues that the magistrate has no commission over souls and that force produces profession, not belief. The argument is available even to an exclusivist. Mill adds harm and the epistemic value of dissent; Rawls explains stability through an overlapping consensus among incompatible doctrines. Aśoka's Rock Edict XII similarly commends concord (*samavāya*) and condemns disparagement without requiring shared theology.

**Coexistence does not follow from truth-agreement.** A metaphysical claim needs an additional political premise about coercion, harm or reciprocity. Thus an exclusivist may respect freedom, while a self-described pluralist may still coerce.

Three qualifications are necessary. Forst's ladder distinguishes permission, coexistence, respect and esteem: majority permission is not equal citizenship. Popper's paradox permits limits only where intolerant actors reject argument and answer with violence; practice may likewise be limited by harm, public order, morality and health. Finally, Shachar's multicultural vulnerability shows that accommodation between groups may empower dominant insiders over women or dissenters.

Indian resources deepen the conclusion. Vivekananda's demand for acceptance rather than toleration exposes condescension, while Gandhi's equal respect rests on fallibilism about one's grasp of truth. Jain many-sidedness supplies an epistemic reason to state one's standpoint explicitly.

Political coexistence therefore depends on respect, reciprocity, exit and harm-limits—not on doctrinal agreement. The claim would fail only if a religious truth itself entailed coercion and no independent moral or political reason could defeat that entailment.

**Why this earns marks:** It proves both halves of the quotation separately, links Western and Indian evidence to the claims they support, faces permission, intolerance and internal-minority objections, and states the defeater that would reverse the conclusion.

#### Original 6 · 20 marks

**Question:** Examine whether the epistemology of deep disagreement strengthens or weakens the case for religious pluralism. *(Answer in about 350 words.)*

**Model answer:**
It does both, and the balance is what makes it examinable.

**The apparatus.** Within a shared framework, the dispute is between **conciliationism** or equal weight (Feldman, Christensen), on which discovering peer disagreement should reduce confidence, and **steadfastness** (Kelly, van Inwagen), on which one may retain a belief because one's own reasoning is part of one's evidence; the **independence principle** — assess the disagreement without relying on the reasoning under dispute — is what conciliationism needs and steadfastness denies. **Deep disagreement**, in Fogelin's technical sense (1985), goes further: the parties do not share the **framework propositions** — Wittgenstein's hinge commitments in *On Certainty* — against which evidence would count, so the same evidence is not evidence for the same thing. Lynch extends the analysis to disagreement about epistemic principles themselves, where any defence is circular.

**Why religion is a paradigm case.** The parties differ over the sources of evidence, the criteria of adequacy — salvation, liberation, cessation — and the framework in which any datum would count.

**Where it weakens pluralism.** Two consequences cut against it. Exclusivism cannot be convicted of irrationality merely for persisting, which removes the easiest charge against it and gives Plantinga's third reply its technical backing. And pluralism cannot be established merely by pointing at persistence — the persistence that seemed to support Hick's first premise is now explained without any pluralist assumption.

**Where it strengthens pluralism.** Two further consequences cut in its favour. The failure of convergence is *predicted* rather than scandalous, so it is weak evidence for relativism. And what *can* be rationally required are the **procedural virtues** — fallibilism, humility, epistemic charity, and willingness to make one's framework explicit — which is precisely the ethic pluralism recommends. Jain conditional predication is the most developed attempt to make framework-relativity explicit inside the proposition.

**The verdict.** Deep disagreement weakens the case for pluralism as a *demonstrated metaphysics* and strengthens it as an *ethic of engagement*. Unresolvability is epistemic, not alethic: one party may still be right, and what fails is our capacity to show which. That is a smaller claim than Hick's, and it is more defensible.

**Why this earns marks:** It distinguishes peer from deep disagreement, names the competing epistemologists and their hinge, derives two consequences against and two for pluralism, uses Jain conditional predication analytically, and closes with a balanced verdict plus its alethic qualification.

### Question-shaped timed-paper compression plan

Under time pressure, protect the element the directive is grading and cut the element that merely displays reading.

| Printed demand | Protect | Cut first |
|---|---|---|
| 2018 Q6(a) distinguish E-I-P | "Who sets the terms" for each of the three | The three conciliar dates |
| 2018 Q6(b) "Truth is one…" Indian context | The context clause and RV 1.164.46 discipline | The Jain apparatus, to one sentence |
| 2019 Q5(b) uniting force | The three conditions | The list of universalist doctrines |
| 2019 Q7(a) central problem | The single-sentence statement of the problem; Plantinga with D'Costa | Hick's four steps, to two clauses |
| 2020 Q5(c) conflict history | Cause / marker / mobiliser / legitimiser | The counter-evidence list, to three items |
| 2020 Q5(e) tolerance | Three components and Forst's ladder | Mill, Rawls and Popper, to one clause |
| 2021 Q5(b) absolute truth possibility | The compatibility mechanism | Two of the three denials of the premise |
| 2021 Q5(c) religious freedom | The internal-minorities paradox | Three of the four justifications |
| 2022 Q7(a) absolute truth → exclusivism | The formal reconstruction with the suppressed premise | One of the three denials |
| 2023 Q8(b) two allegations | The explicit separation of the two charges | The four-role analysis, to one clause |
| 2024 Q5(b) notion of absolute truth | The four-way separation | One of the two consequences |
| 2025 Q7(a) Vedāntic pluralism | Vivekananda's own theses and the contested assimilation objection | The Jain addition, to one clause |

### Self-scoring and next steps

Score each attempt on five bands. **Weak** — E-I-P named, "all religions lead to the same God". **Average** — E-I-P correctly distinguished, Hick and Vivekananda present, but exclusivism presented only as bigotry. **Good** — truth, salvation, access and coexistence separated and one objection answered. **Strong** — Plantinga stated and met; RV 1.164.46 and Cyprian handled correctly; many-sidedness used as a logic. **Top** — all of that, plus one discriminating distinction (Forst's ladder, Heim against Hick, D'Costa's charge, or epistemic-versus-alethic unresolvability) and an explicit acknowledgement of the hard contradictions pluralism cannot dissolve.

If two consecutive attempts land below **Good**, return to Sessions 1, 14 and 15 before attempting another 20-marker: the fault is almost always a merged axis or an unconceded contradiction rather than missing content.

### Independence note

Every model answer, original question, diagnostic item and option explanation in this package is **independent learner practice**. None reproduces an official mark scheme, and none should be presented as one. Only the twelve previous-year questions are reproduced verbatim, from the audited repository ledger, and they are reproduced for practice routing.

## OPTIONAL ADVANCED DEPTH — NOT REQUIRED FOR A CORE ANSWER

Everything above is sufficient for a complete answer at every printed demand. The four contrasts below are **second-order debate**: they sharpen evaluation in a 15- or 20-marker and are never a prerequisite for understanding or writing the core answer. Add **one** advanced move to a 15-marker, or **one to two** to a 20-marker.

**[FACT] Promotion note.** The Advanced dossier's promotion ledger of **15 August 2026** records **deep disagreement about Hick's Real** as promoted out of Advanced and into the owner. It is therefore taught in Session 16 as core, and is not repeated here as optional enrichment.

### A1 — Hickian pluralism against the hard-incompatibility critique

**Competing positions.** Hick relocates religions as valid responses to the Real; critics stress that contradictory truth-claims cannot all be true in the same sense.
**Strongest extra objection → counter-reply.** *Objection:* the unknowable Real is too thin to ground determinate religions. *Reply:* Hick presents a second-order hypothesis about transformation rather than a new first-order creed — **though revisionism remains a live charge**.
**Safe use.** As an evaluative paragraph *after* the E-I-P triad has been laid out clearly. It does not replace the exposition.

### A2 — Vedāntic universalism against the assimilation critique

**Competing positions.** Vivekananda and Radhakrishnan read religions as converging paths; critics say this absorbs others into a neo-Vedāntic frame.
**Strongest extra objection → counter-reply.** *Objection:* "all paths" may covertly privilege Vedānta. *Reply:* defenders hold that convergence need not erase distinct practices, only deny monopoly over the Real.
**Safe use.** To show maturity in a 2025-style Vedāntic pluralism answer. Session 11 already carries the core form of this exchange; the advanced move is only the sharper statement of the privileging worry.

### A3 — Deep disagreement against comparative learning

**Competing positions.** One line holds that traditions are too conceptually different for neat synthesis; another holds that comparison can still produce disciplined mutual learning without forced sameness.
**Strongest extra objection → counter-reply.** *Objection:* translation across traditions distorts their self-understanding. *Reply:* comparative work can preserve disagreement while identifying partial overlap, analogy or shared ethical stakes.
**Safe use.** One sentence in a conclusion, to avoid superficial "all religions are the same" rhetoric.

### A4 — Many-sidedness against relativism, as a second-order contest

**Competing positions.** Jain many-sidedness allows standpoint-conditioned truth without collapsing into "anything goes".
**Strongest extra objection → counter-reply.** *Objection:* if every claim is only conditional, no claim has full authority. *Reply:* Jain logic regulates predication by standpoint and object-aspect; it disciplines absolutism rather than abolishing truth.
**Safe use.** Particularly safe in Indian-context answers on absolute truth and conflict. The core form of the doctrine is in Session 13; this is the second-order authority worry only.

### What not to over-study

Do not turn the answer into comparative-religion sociology or global conflict commentary detached from philosophical positions. The clause is about **theories of religious diversity and the problem of absolute truth**, not about the descriptive history or present evaluation of particular communities.

### Advanced source trail

✅ John Hick, *An Interpretation of Religion*; ✅ Swami Vivekananda, lectures on universal religion and the Chicago addresses; ✅ S. Radhakrishnan, *The Hindu View of Life*; ✅ Gandhi on equal respect for religions; ✅ Jain materials on many-sidedness and conditional predication; ✅ contemporary comparative-theology literature where needed as a secondary check.

---

## CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

Twenty-one topic-specific cards, one per taught session. These are compressed revision notes, not a second run of the teaching prose.

### 1. Four axes, and the two illicit slides

- Diversity becomes a problem only where **determinate truth-claims + determinate salvation + a demand for consistency** meet.
- **Truth (alethic)** · **Salvation (soteriological)** · **Epistemic access** · **Political coexistence** — four independent questions.
- Slide A (✗): "we coexist peacefully" ⇒ "all religions are equally true".
- Slide B (✗): "there is one absolute Truth" ⇒ "therefore I am an exclusivist".
- Pluralism ≠ relativism: it can deny equal truth, retain non-contradiction, and still deny exhaustive possession.
- Tolerance is a civic ethic and is **available to an exclusivist**.
- Answer habit: name the axis in the first sentence.

### 2. Exclusivism, and the attribution chain

- One revelation carries decisive truth and/or salvific authority; appeals to **determinate revelation + non-contradiction**.
- Presupposition: **privileged warrant**, and salvation specifiable enough for comparison.
- Tag: *extra ecclesiam nulla salus* = **later standard phrasing**. Cyprian (d. 258), *Ep.* 73: *salus extra ecclesiam non est*; also *De Ecclesiae Catholicae Unitate* 6.
- Conciliar hardening: Lateran IV **1215** · *Unam Sanctam* **1302** · Florence **1442**.
- Qualification: Vatican II **1965** — *Lumen Gentium* 16, *Nostra Aetate* ⇒ decisive move toward inclusivism.
- Strength: determinate content preserved. Weakness: privileged warrant; **contingency of birth**.
- Exclusivism about truth ≠ intolerance about practice (Locke).

### 3. Inclusivism

- Grants **partial truth** and possible salvation beyond the home tradition, interpreted **through** it.
- Argument: determinate revelation **+ universality of grace**. Normative priority retained.
- Model: Rahner's **anonymous Christians** — a claim about grace, not covert membership.
- Differentia: *others are included on terms set by the privileged tradition*.
- Objection: patronising. Reply: every judgement uses a framework; the test is corrigibility and respect for self-descriptions.
- Residual cost: **asymmetry** — the home tradition is never redescribed in the other's categories.

### 4. Pluralism, defined by its denial

- Affirms: several traditions are **authentic responses**, or attain **different real ends**.
- Denies: any finite tradition **exhaustively possesses** the absolute.
- Not relativism (there *is* a truth of the matter) · not indifferentism · not syncretism · not perennialist identity.
- Acknowledged weakness: dilution of specifics; the **Real *an sich*** is unknowable.
- Relativism criticism → reply: **equal dignity / salvific efficacy ≠ identical propositional truth**.
- Self-reference criticism → reply: defeasible hypothesis; rejoinder: it still judges first-order traditions.

### 5. Hick I — the Copernican revolution

- Centre moves from a tradition or incarnation to the **Real**.
- Four steps: (1) persistent, birth-linked diversity; (2) transformation across traditions; (3) equal divine concern resists arbitrary exclusivism; (4) therefore one Real through different schemes.
- Premise 3 is **theological** ⇒ unavailable against a non-theistic exclusivist.
- Conclusion is a **possibility claim** by inference to the best explanation, not a proof.
- The Real is **transcategorial**: personal / non-personal predicates attach to manifestations.
- Sources: *An Interpretation of Religion*; *God and the Universe of Faiths*.

### 6. Hick II — the Kantian basis

- Real *an sich* : phenomena :: noumenon : appearance; conceptual schemes function as categories.
- **Departure from Kant:** the noumenal Real is treated as **causally related** to religious experience.
- Presuppositions: the Kantian distinction extends religiously; transformation is comparable across traditions.
- **Noumenal objection:** existence, causality and goodness become illicit predications.
- Reply: predication is **formal, not substantive** ⇒ consistency preserved, informativeness reduced.
- Indian comparator: Advaita has a **built-in** *nirguṇa*/*saguṇa* two-level semantics; Hick must construct one.

### 7. Hick III — the transformation criterion

- **Self-centredness → Reality-centredness**, evidenced by saints and moral fruits.
- Scope: authenticity of a **tradition**; it does **not** make contradictory doctrines jointly true.
- Proposed because doctrinal comparison lacks a neutral adjudicating framework.
- Three objections at three levels: (a) thin Real; (b) **self-referential / parity**; (c) **identity and mission** — hear it, do not declare it solved.
- Conflicting ends: communion, non-dual knowledge, cessation are not obviously one end. Reply: family resemblance / transformation; critics call it **flattening**.
- Coarse-grained: cannot discriminate between two traditions that both produce saints.

### 8. Plantinga and D'Costa

- Charges: **arbitrariness, arrogance, irrationality**.
- Replies: the pluralist holds a contested belief too; believing what others deny is the ordinary philosophical condition; disagreement **reduces** but does not extinguish warrant.
- Counter 1: establishes **permissibility, not truth**. Counter 2: **contingency of birth** untouched.
